For n=512, 512 kernel launches overhead ~ few microseconds each => ~2.5ms overhead, total work ~45M FLOPs => maybe ~1ms. Not great but okay. For scalability to larger n, kernel launch overhead becomes negligible relative. But we want maximum performance.\n\n2. Right-looking blocked algorithm with tile size (e.g., 32 or 64). Right-looking within panel can be done.\n\nThis is the best performance but requires careful implementation.\n\nAlternative: Use cuBLAS for GEMM and TRSM (dependency from CUDA toolkit) and custom kernels for diagonal factorization. This can be one kernel with grid over rows and columns, but within block columns dependency: for j in block, L[i,j] = (A[i,j] - sum_{k<block start} L[i,k]*L[j,k] - sum_{k=block start}^{j-1} L[i,k]*L[j,k]) / L[j,j]. Alternative: compute the panel with a single thread block that loops over j (columns) and parallelizes over rows; per column uses all threads to compute dot products. This is a triangular solve of panel (rows x TS) with TS columns sequentially—cost O(rows*TS^2/2) in one thread block, underutilizing GPU for large rows. But panel work is small relative to trailing update (O(n^3) overall, panel portion O(n^2*TS)). For n large, panel is less.\n\n   - Trailing update: A[i,j] -= sum_{k in block} L[i,k]*L[j,k] for i,j in trailing. This is a GEMM of size (n - (b+1)TS) x TS times TS x same. Many thread blocks, tiled shared memory. This dominates. Good.\n\nSo per block: 1 diag factor kernel (maybe 1 block), panel solve sequential columns using maybe one or a few kernel launches, and one GEMM kernel. Bank = c*33 mod 32 = c mod 32 = (tx*4) mod32 = 0,4,...60 mod32 -> tx 0..7 gives 0,4,...28; tx 8..15 gives 0,4,...28 again => 2-way conflicts for Bs. Could pad stride 34? Then bank = c*34 mod32 = 2c mod32 = tx*8 mod32 -> 0,8,16,24 repeated 4x => worse. Stride 33 gives 2-way? c*33 mod32 = c mod32; c=tx*4 so 4tx mod32 -> period 8 -> 2-way. Not terrible. Could use swizzle or choose 2x2 per thread. Alternatively use stride 32 (no padding): c*32 mod32 =0 all same bank -> 16-way conflict. Bad. The upper entries of diagonal block remain original; not used.\n\nImplementation of factorDiagonalBlock:\n```\n#define TS 32\n__global__ void factorDiagonalBlockKernel(double* A, int n, int diagStart, int diagSize,\n                                          int* failFlag, int* failIdx) {\n    __shared__ double s[TS][TS+1];\n    int tid = threadIdx.x; // use 1024 threads? blockDim.x = TS*TS = 1024\n    // load\n    for (int idx = tid; idx < diagSize*diagSize; idx += blockDim.x) {\n       int r = idx / diagSize; int c = idx % diagSize;\n       s[r][c] = A[(size_t)(diagStart+r)*n + diagStart+c];\n    }\n    __syncthreads();\n    for (int j=0;j<diagSize;j++) {\n       // compute diagonal\n       if (tid==0) {\n          double sum=0;\n          for (int k=0;k<j;k++) sum += s[j][k]*s[j][k];\n          double val = s[j][j] - sum;\n          if (val <= 0.0) {\n             if (*failFlag == 0) { *failFlag = 1; *failIdx = diagStart+j; }\n             // Avoid NaN? set s[j][j] = sqrt(fabs(val))? but if val<=0, continue\n             s[j][j] = sqrt(val > 0.0 ? val : 1.0); // set 1 to avoid div0? but results invalid anyway\n          } else {\n             s[j][j] = sqrt(val);\n          }\n       }\n       __syncthreads();\n       // update column below\n       double ljj = s[j][j];\n       for (int i = j+1 + tid; i < diagSize; i += blockDim.x) {\n          double sum=0;\n          for (int k=0;k<j;k++) sum += s[i][k]*s[j][k];\n          s[i][j] = (s[i][j]-sum)/ljj;\n       }\n       __syncthreads();\n    }\n    // write lower triangle back\n    for (int idx = tid; idx < diagSize*diagSize; idx += blockDim.x) {\n       int r = idx / diagSize; int c = idx % diagSize;\n       if (r >= c) A[(size_t)(diagStart+r)*n + diagStart+c] = s[r][c];\n    }\n}\n```\nUsing `for i = j+1+tid; i < diagSize; i += blockDim.x`. A[(size_t)gi*n + diagStart + kk] : 0.0;\n   }\n   // Load Bs similarly with colTile\n   for (int idx = tid; idx < G_TILE*K; idx += 256) {\n      int c = idx / K;\n      int kk = idx % K;\n      int gj = base + colTile*G_TILE + c;\n      Bs[c][kk] = (gj < n) ? A[(size_t)gj*n + diagStart + kk] : 0.0;\n   }\n   __syncthreads();\n   int r0 = threadIdx.y * G_PER;\n   int c0 = threadIdx.x * G_PER;\n   double acc[G_PER][G_PER] = {0};\n   for (int kk=0; kk<K; ++kk) {\n      double a0 = As[r0+0][kk]; ... a3\n      double b0 = Bs[c0+0][kk]; ...\n      acc[0][0] += a0*b0; ...\n   }\n   for (int rr=0; rr<G_PER; ++rr) {\n      int r = r0+rr;\n      int gi = base + rowTile*G_TILE + r;\n      if (gi >= n) continue; // could break\n      for (int cc=0; cc<G_PER; ++cc) {\n         int c = c0+cc;\n         int gj = base + colTile*G_TILE + c;\n         if (gj >= n) continue;\n         if (colTile == rowTile && c > r) continue; // skip upper within diag tile\n         A[(size_t)gi*n + gj] -= acc[rr][cc];\n      }\n   }\n}\n```\nWait for off-diagonal tiles colTile<rowTile, all c,r in tile needed? Then panel solve is a GEMM: for each row i and col j in block, L[i,j] = sum_{k=j..K-1} A[i,k] * invL[j,k]? Let's derive: L11 * X = A21^T where X = L21^T (K x m). X = L11^{-1} A21^T. So L21 = A21 * (L11^{-1})^T. If we have invL = L11^{-1}, then L21[i,j] = sum_k A21[i,k] * invL[j,k] (since (invL)^T[k,j]=invL[j,k]) for k from j..K-1? Because invL is lower triangular, invL[j,k]=0 for k>j, so sum k<=j. Wait L11 lower triangular, inverse lower triangular: invL[j,k] nonzero for k<=j. Then L21[i,j] = sum_{k=0..j} A21[i,k]*invL[j,k]? Let's check dimensions: A21 (m x K) times invL^T (K x K) where invL^T is upper triangular. (A21 * invL^T)[i,j] = sum_k A21[i,k] * invL^T[k,j] = sum_k A21[i,k]*invL[j,k]. invL[j,k] nonzero for k<=j. So sum k=0..j. But A21[i,k] includes original A values for all k in block, not yet solved. Does this work? Yes, the formula directly computes L21 from the already-updated A21 and invL. This replaces the triangular solve with a small GEMM of size m x K x K. It can be parallelized over (i,j) tiles with all data in shared, and no sequential dependency across j! Great.\n\nComputing invL: We can compute it in the diagonal factor kernel and write it to a workspace, or store it in global memory in the lower triangle? We need L11 itself for GEMM trailing update (L21) and for the next blocks? The panel GEMM needs invL. The trailing GEMM needs L21, not invL. The diagonal block L11 is needed only for output. We can store invL in a separate device buffer of size K*K per block, or overwrite the diagonal block's upper triangle (unused) with invL? The upper triangle of the diagonal block is not used and will be zeroed at the end. We could store invL in the upper triangle of the diagonal block (row j, cols > j?) But invL is lower triangular, so could store its transpose (upper) in the upper triangle. Specifically, store invL[j,k] (k<=j) into A[diagStart+k?]. We need convenient access. Alternatively allocate a small workspace `double* dInv` of size ALGO_TS*ALGO_TS (32*32) reused per block. We can allocate one workspace buffer on device (or include in dA extra space). Since no new files, allocate with cudaMalloc or use a static global device array? We can allocate `dWork` of size ALGO_TS*ALGO_TS doubles. Pass to factor kernel and panel kernel. Factor kernel computes invL and writes to dWork (row-major K x K, lower triangular maybe full). Panel kernel uses dWork. Then no need for `diag` shared in panel except invL loaded. But the panel GEMM formula uses invL and A21 (original block columns). It doesn't need L11 diagonal. Great.\n\nWait, does the panel GEMM formula require A21 to be the updated block (previous contributions subtracted)? Yes, A21 in global has been updated by previous blocks. Good.\n\nCompute invL in factor kernel: After factoring L in shared s, compute inverse of lower triangular L. Since K<=32, we can compute column by column or use forward substitution. The inverse of a lower triangular matrix is lower triangular. For each column c (0..K-1), solve L * x = e_c. Since L lower triangular, x[i]=0 for i<c, x[c]=1/L[c][c], and for i>c: x[i] = -(1/L[i][i]) * sum_{k=c..i-1} L[i][k]*x[k]. This is sequential over i for each c. Total O(K^3/6) ~ 5k ops for K=32, negligible. Can be done by tid0 serial or parallel over c with one thread per column? Each column independent. We could use K threads, each c computes x sequentially over i. K<=32, so one warp with lane=c handles one column. Each lane loops i=c..K-1 and accumulates over k, needing x[k] which it computed earlier (in registers? dynamic array). Could use shared inv[K][K]. For each c (lane), set inv[c][c]=1/L[c][c], then for i=c+1..K-1 compute sum over k=c..i-1 of L[i][k]*inv[k][c], then inv[i][c] = -sum/L[i][i]. Since each lane c works on its own column, no cross-lane dependencies. Store inv in shared. Then write to dWork. This can run in the same factor kernel after L computed. Use warp 0 lanes 0..K-1. Need __syncthreads after L factorization, then compute inv (only warp 0 active, others wait), then write dWork. Serial per lane ~K^2/2 ~512 ops, small. Could be ~ a few us due to serial double chains. Alternatively use more threads: one warp per column? But K<=32, one warp per column c, each warp computes x[i] with lanes over k reduction. That would be 32 warps = 1024 threads, but we use 256 threads (8 warps). Could use 8 warps to handle columns in groups. But inverse cost is negligible relative.\n\nActually, maybe we don't even need explicit inverse. We can use the panel GEMM with L11 and a forward substitution per row? No, inverse is fine.\n\nThen panel solve kernel becomes a GEMM-like update: compute L21[i,j] = sum_{k=0..j} A21[i,k] * invL[j,k] for i in [diagEnd,n), j in [diagStart,diagEnd). But note A21[i,k] is the original (updated) A in global, and invL[j,k] is in dWork. The result overwrites A[i,j] for j in current block. We must be careful: for a given row i, computing all j uses A[i,k] for k in current block, which are still original values; we must not overwrite them before all j for that row are computed. If we compute each (i,j) in parallel and write immediately, other threads computing different j for same i may read the overwritten A[i,k]. Race! We need to either load the whole A21 row block into shared/registers before writing, or use a separate output buffer. We can design a tiled kernel where each block handles a tile of rows (e.g., 64 rows) and all K columns. Load A[i,k] for rows in tile and k<K into shared (64x32 = 2048 doubles = 16KB), load invL (32x32 = 8KB) into shared, sync, compute outputs for all (i,j) in shared or registers, then write back. This is like a small GEMM with M=rows tile, N=K, K=K. With 256 threads, each thread handles e.g. 64*32/256=8 outputs. No race because input is in shared. This kernel has high parallelism (tiles of rows) and no sequential j dependency. It should be much faster than the warp-per-row solve, and also simpler.\n\nActually, we can use the same `updateTrailingKernel`-style GEMM? But dimensions: M=rows, N=K<=32, K=K. validateCholesky(const std::vector<double>& L, const std::vector<double>& A_orig, const size_t n) {\n    // Validate by computing L * L^T and comparing with original matrix\n    \n    std::vector<double> reconstructed(n * n);\n    \n    // Compute L * L^T\n    for (size_t i = 0; i < n; ++i) {\n        for (size_t j = 0; j < n; ++j) {\n            double sum = 0.0;\n            for (size_t k = 0; k < n; ++k) {\n                sum += L[i * n + k] * L[j * n + k];\n            }\n            reconstructed[i * n + j] = sum;\n        }\n    }\n    \n    // Compare with original\n
